Ownership Refinement Types for Pointer Arithmetic and Nested Arrays
Yusuke Fujiwara, Yusuke Matsushita, Kohei Suenaga, Atsushi Igarashi

Abstract
Tanaka et al. proposed a type system for verifying functional correctness properties of programs that use arrays and pointer arithmetic. Their system extends ConSORT —a type system combining fractional ownership and refinement types for imperative program verification—with support for pointer arithmetic. Their idea was to extend fractional ownership so that it can depend on an array index. Their formulation, however, does not handle nested arrays, which are essential for representing practical data structures such as matrices. We extend Tanaka et al.’s type system to support nested arrays by generalizing the notion of ownership to be able to refer to the indices of the outer arrays and prove the soundness of the extended type system. We have implemented a verifier based on the proposed type system and demonstrated that it can verify the correctness of programs that manipulate nested arrays, which were beyond the reach of Tanaka et al.

1
Introduction

Type-based automated program verification has been a popular methodology for
making software reliable.
Among various type systems proposed so far, recent years have seen significant
success of
refinement type
systems

[
25
,
19
]
—type systems that can constrain values using predicates called
refinement predicates
—to guarantee properties that cannot be expressed with base types alone.
For example, a refinement type system can express the type
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu>0\}
of positive integers using the refinement predicate
\nu>0
, which is more expressive than its simple type
\mathbf{int}
.
Refinement type systems have been applied to various types of programs including functional programs
[
19
,
48
]
,
object-oriented programs
[
43
]
, and
smart contracts
[
30
,
32
]
.

However, applying refinement types to imperative programs with pointers and aliasing has been less popular than for other kinds of programs.
A naive way of introducing refinement types for an imperative language would be to incorporate reference types.
For example, such a type system would have type
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu>0\}\TREF
for pointers to positive integers.
The type system would be designed to be flow-sensitive: typing rules would be designed as if the type of each variable were updated by each statement.
For example, if
x
has type
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu>0\}\TREF
just before a statement
x:=-1
that updates the memory cell pointed to by
x
to
-1
, then the type of
x
just after this statement would be
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu=-1\}\TREF
.

One major challenge in this approach is the
strong update
problem, explained below.
Suppose pointers
x
and
y
both have type
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu>0\}\TREF
just before a statement
x:=1
.
Then, the type of
x
after this statement is updated to a type like
\{\nu\COL\mathbf{int}\mid\nu=1\}\TREF
.
However, naively updating only the refinement type of
x
is not enough in general; if
x
and
y
are the same pointer, then the type of
y
must also be updated since the memory cell pointed to by
y
is updated by this statement.

To address this issue, Toman et al.
[
46
]
proposed a type system
ConSORT
.
Their type system, instead of conducting static must-alias analysis, constrains aliases based on types.
Concretely, their reference type is augmented with information called
(fractional) ownership
, with which their type system imposes the following invariants for each memory cell:

•

there is at most one pointer that (1) can be used for updating and reading the memory cell and (2) is associated with a non-trivial (i.e., not
\top
) refinement predicate; and

•

there may be additional pointers that (1) can be used only for reading from the memory cell and (2) are associated with a non-trivial refinement predicate.

With these constraints, the type system does not need to handle aliasing in a strong update, since it is guaranteed that there are no aliases reading non-trivial information from the updated memory cell.

Later, Tanaka et al.
[
44
]
extended
ConSORT

to support pointer arithmetic, enabling the verification of programs that manipulate integer arrays and perform pointer arithmetic.
Their key idea is to extend the notion of ownership to
(fractional)
ownership functions
from array indices to fractional ownerships.
With this extension, their type system can reason about the properties of array elements in an index-sensitive way.
Pointer arithmetic is reflected as index-shifting operations over the
ownership function at the type system’s level.

⬇

(

,

){

Figure 1:
A motivating example.

In this paper, we extend Tanaka et al.’s type system to handle another kind of programs that they do not support: programs manipulating
nested arrays
, which frequently arise in programs that manipulate matrices and tensors.
Figure
1
is the motivating example that we use to illustrate our contribution.
The function
initMatrix
takes an integer
n
and a pointer
\mathit{pp}
to an array of length
n
, and allocates an integer array to each element of the array pointed to by
pp
so that
pp
points to a matrix represented by a two-dimensional array; concretely, the matrix is initialized so that
\mathit{pp}+i
points to an array with length
n-i
, all of whose elements are set to
n
.
To this end, if
n>0
,
initMatrix
allocates an array of length
n
, binds
s
to a pointer to it (Line
13
), calls
\texttt{initArray}(n,n,s)
that initializes all the elements of the array pointed to by
s
of length
n
to
n
(Line
14
), writes
s
to the memory cell pointed to by
\mathit{pp}
(Line
15
), and recursively calls
\texttt{initMatrix}(n-1,\mathit{pp}+1)
(Line
16
).

Verifying the above specification of
initMatrix
using the idea of ownership functions by Tanaka et al. requires the ownership of an inner array to be dependent not only on its index but also on the index of the outer array.
However, Tanaka et al.’s type system does not support such dependency; hence, their type system cannot handle the program in Figure
1
.

Our extension, based on Tanaka et al.’s type system, introduces
(fractional) ownership terms
to describe a function from array indices to ownerships.
Intuitively, the ownership of the inner array of the matrix pointed to by
\mathit{pp}
after the execution of
initMatrix
must satisfy the following: Via pointer
\mathit{pp}
, the memory
cell
(*(\mathit{pp}+x_{2}))+x_{1}
can be updated if
0\leq x_{2}\leq n-1\land 0\leq x_{1}\leq n-x_{2}-1
, where

x_{1}
represents the index of an inner array and

x_{2}
represents the index of an outer array.
Using an ownership term, this constraint is described as

(0\leq x_{2}\leq n-1\land 0\leq x_{1}\leq n-x_{2}-1)\produces 1
; here,
\produces 1
expresses the ownership for reading and updating the memory cell.
Note that this ownership term for an inner array refers to the index
x_{2}
of the outer array.

Contribution.

We formally define an imperative language and its type system equipped with ownership terms.
We also prove the soundness of the type system: a well-typed program does not cause assertion failures, out-of-bounds array accesses, or null-pointer dereferences.
We also design a type inference procedure and implement a prototype verifier based on the procedure.
We apply our verifier to several programs that manipulate nested arrays, and we
demonstrate that it successfully and efficiently verifies the functional
correctness of these programs, which was not possible with Tanaka et al.’s type system.

Although our motivating examples primarily use two-dimensional arrays for clarity of presentation, our type system and implementation support nested arrays of arbitrary depth. In particular, we demonstrate this generality by verifying programs that manipulate three- and four-dimensional arrays (Section
5
).

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
defines the target imperative language and its operational semantics.
Section
3
presents the proposed type system and states its soundness.
Section
4
describes a template-based type inference procedure.
Section
5
reports on the experimental results, comparing the performance of our implemented verifier and evaluating the verification capabilities for programs involving nested arrays.
Section
6
discusses the applicability of our approach to mainstream programming languages and possible extensions to more general heap structures.
Section
7
describes related work.
Section
8
concludes the paper.
We omit some definitions and proofs; they can be found in Appendix.

2
Target Language

2.1
Syntax

Figure
2
shows the syntax of the target
language.
The set of variables
\mathbf{Var}
is ranged over by
w
,
x
,
y
, and
z
. The
metavariable
n
represents integer constants;
f
represents function
names;
\varphi
represents first-order logic formulae over integers.
The metavariable
e
represents expressions;
\mathit{fd}
represents
function definitions;
D
represents a finite set of function
definitions;
\mathit{P}
represents programs; and
\tau^{-}
represents
simple types, consisting of integer and reference types. An expression of
the form
\LET x=\ldots\IN e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
binds
x
in
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
. We write

[y_{{\mathrm{1}}}/x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,y_{\mathit{n}}/x_{\mathit{n}}]
for simultaneous capture-avoiding
substitution of variable
y_{i}
for variable
x_{i}
(for

1\leq i\leq n
). A substitution is also denoted by
\theta
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}\tau^{-}&::=&\mathbf{int}\mid\tau^{-}\TREF\\
e&::=&x\mid\LET x=n\IN e\mid\LET x=\mathbf{null}\IN e\mid\LET x=y\mathop{-}z\IN e\\
&\mid&\IFNP x\THEN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}\ELSE e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mid\LET x=f(y_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,y_{\mathit{n}})\IN e\mid\LET x=e_{{\mathrm{0}}}\IN e_{{\mathrm{1}}}\\
&\mid&\LET x=\ALLOC y:\tau^{-}\TREF\IN e\mid\LET x=\ast y\IN e\mid x\WRITE y\SEQ e\mid\LET x=y\boxplus z\IN e\\
&\mid&\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ e\mid\mathbf{alias}(x=\ast y)\SEQ e\mid\mathbf{assert}(\varphi)\SEQ e\\
\mathit{fd}&::=&f\mapsto(x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}})\,e\\
D&::=&\{\mathit{fd_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\,...\,,\mathit{fd_{\mathit{n}}}\}\\
\mathit{P}&::=&\left\langle{D,e}\right\rangle\\
\end{array}

Figure 2:
Syntax of the target language.

In this language, constants and the value of every intermediate computation are named with
\LET
.
We informally explain the meaning of expression forms.

•

\IFNP x\THEN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}\ELSE e_{{\mathrm{1}}}
evaluates

e_{{\mathrm{0}}}
if
x
is not a positive integer; it evaluates
e_{{\mathrm{1}}}

otherwise.

•

\LET x=f(y_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,y_{\mathit{n}})\IN e
calls function

f
with actual arguments
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
, binds
x
to the value
returned from
f
, and evaluates
e
. Without loss of
generality, we assume
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
if
i\neq j
.

•

\LET x=\ALLOC y:\tau^{-}\TREF\IN e
allocates an array of
size
y
, binds
x
to the pointer to the 0-th memory cell of the
array, and evaluates
e
.
We assume that the simple type of
x
(i.e.,
\tau^{-}\TREF)
is
annotated.
As shown below, the type annotation determines the initial values stored in the allocated array.

If a nested
\mathbf{ref}
type is specified as
\tau^{-}
, then
x
is bound to a pointer to an array of length
y
whose elements
are initialized to
\mathbf{null}
.
Each cell is expected to be initialized with a pointer to
another (nested) array before it is accessed.

If
y
is not positive, an empty array will be allocated and a
dangling pointer will be returned. However, the type system
prevents such a pointer from being accessed.

•

\LET x=\ast y\IN e
reads from the memory cell pointed to by the pointer

y
, binds
x
to the read value, and evaluates

e
.

•

x\WRITE y\SEQ e
updates the memory cell pointed to by

x
with the value
y
and evaluates
e
.

•

\LET x=y\boxplus z\IN e
is for pointer
arithmetic; it binds
x
to the pointer obtained by shifting
y
by
offset
z
and evaluates
e
.

•

\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ e
(resp.,
\mathbf{alias}(x=\ast y)\SEQ e
) evaluates
e
if
x=y\boxplus z

(resp.,
x=\ast y
) holds; otherwise, it raises an exception
indicating that the alias relationship does not hold.
Operationally, this expression works as a runtime check for the alias relationship; if the alias relationship does not hold, execution is safely terminated by raising an exception.
Statically, this expression serves as a hint to the type checker: it declares the programmer’s intended aliasing relationship between pointers; the type checker is allowed to redistribute the ownership between the pointers
x
and
y\boxplus z
(resp.,
\ast y
); see
T-AliasAddPtr
and
T-AliasDeref
in
\cref
sec:typesystem for details.
As in previous work on fractional ownership
[
44
,
46
,
42
,
41
]
,
our type system guarantees the absence of errors due to runtime assertion failures, out-of-bounds accesses, and null-pointer dereferences; an incorrect alias expression leads to the explicit error state
\mathbf{AliasFail}
rather than to undefined behavior
(see
\cref
sec:opsem and Theorem
3.1
).

•

\mathbf{assert}(\varphi)\SEQ e
evaluates
e
if the formula
\varphi

holds; otherwise, the evaluation gets stuck due to an assertion failure, which means that
the program goes into an unsafe state. A well-typed program in our
type system is guaranteed not to cause this error.

A function definition

\mathit{fd}
is of the form
f\mapsto(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})e
where
f
is
the name of the defined function,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are the names of
the parameters, and
e
is the body of the function. We assume that

x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
are pairwise distinct.
Finally, a program
\mathit{P}
is a pair of a set of function definitions

D
and a main expression
e
.

2.2
Operational Semantics

We define the operational semantics of the language.

First, we give the syntax of
pointer values
and
values
, ranged over by
pv
and
\mathit{v}
, respectively.

a\in\mathcal{A}\qquad pv::=(a,i)\mid\mathbf{null}\qquad\mathit{v}::=n\mid pv

An
address
is represented by a pair
(a,i)
of a base address

a
, which is an element of a fixed set
\mathcal{A}
, and an
offset
i
, which is an integer. Two addresses
(a,i)
and
(a^{\prime},i^{\prime})

are equal if and only if
a=a^{\prime}
and
i=i^{\prime}
.
A
pointer value

pv
is either an address or the null pointer
\mathbf{null}
.
Unlike other type systems using fractional ownership
[
42
]
,
in which
\mathbf{null}
is used to represent the end of a linked list,
our type system gives
\mathbf{null}
zero ownership, preventing
access to the null pointer statically. A
value

\mathit{v}
is either
a pointer value or an integer.

The small-step operational semantics of our language is given as a
rewriting relation between configurations, ranged over by
C
, of the form

\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},e}\right\rangle
or an error state
\mathbf{AliasFail}
.
Here,
R
is a map from a finite subset of
\mathbf{Var}
to the set of values,
modeling a register file,
and
H
is a (partial) map from the set of addresses to the set of values,
modeling a heap.
We write
\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})
and
\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})
for the domain of
R
and
H
, respectively.
If
\left\{{x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}}\right\}\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})=\emptyset
, we write
R\left\{{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\mapsto\mathit{v_{{\mathrm{1}}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\mapsto\mathit{v_{\mathit{n}}}}\right\}
for the map obtained by
extending
R
with new bindings that map
x_{\mathit{i}}
to
\mathit{v_{\mathit{i}}}
(for
1\leq i\leq n
).
Similarly, if
\left\{{(a,0),\ldots,(a,n)}\right\}\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})=\emptyset
, we write
\mathit{H}\{(a,0)\mapsto\mathit{v_{{\mathrm{0}}}},\ldots,(a,n)\mapsto\mathit{v_{\mathit{n}}}\}
for an extension of
H
with

(a,i)\mapsto v_{i}
(for
0\leq i\leq n
) and, if
(a,n)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})
, we write
\mathit{H}\{(a,n)\hookleftarrow\mathit{v}\}
for the heap that is
identical to
H
except that the address
(a,n)
is now mapped to the
value
v
.

\Cref

fig:opsem gives the excerpt of the rules to define
the reduction step
\longrightarrow_{D}
, parameterized by a set of
function definitions
D
.
The full set of rules is given in
\cref
sec:opsemFull.
We write
\mathbb{Z}
for the set of integers.

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}x^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET x=\mathbf{null}\IN e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\\
\left\langle{R\{x^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{null}\},H,[x^{\prime}/x]e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-LetNull
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{R}(y)\,=\,pv\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptx^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptR(z)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{r}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET x=y\boxplus z\IN e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\\
\left\langle{\mathit{R}\{x^{\prime}\mapsto pv\boxplus\mathit{R}(z)\},\mathit{H},[x^{\prime}/x]e}\right\rangle\end{array}\end{array}}

(
Rs-AddPtr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}(a,0)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptx^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptH^{\prime}=\mathit{H}\{(a,0),\ldots,(a,\mathit{R}(y)\,-\,1)\mapsto 0\}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET x=\ALLOC y:\mathbf{int}\TREF\IN e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\left\langle{\mathit{R}\{x^{\prime}\mapsto(a,0)\},\mathit{H^{\prime}},[x^{\prime}/x]e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-MkArrayIntref
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}(a,0)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptx^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{R})\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{H^{\prime}}=\mathit{H}\{(a,0),\ldots,(a,\mathit{R}(y)\,-\,1)\mapsto\mathbf{null}\}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptR(y)\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET x=\ALLOC y:(\tau^{-}\TREF)\TREF\IN e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\left\langle{\mathit{R}\{x^{\prime}\mapsto(a,0)\},\mathit{H^{\prime}},[x^{\prime}/x]e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-MkArrayNestedref
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{R}(y)\,=\,pv\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptR(z)\in\mathbb{Z}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{R}(x)=pv\boxplus\mathit{R}(z)\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-AliasAddPtr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{R}(y)\,=\,pv\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptR(z)\in\mathbb{Z}\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\mathit{R}(x)\neq pv\boxplus\mathit{R}(z)\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\mathbf{AliasFail}\end{array}}

(
Rs-AliasAddPtrFail
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\mathit{H}(\mathit{R}(y))\,=\,\mathit{R}(x)\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{alias}(x=\ast y)\SEQ e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-AliasDeref
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}R(y)=\mathbf{null}\mbox{ or }\mathit{H}(\mathit{R}(y))\,\neq\,\mathit{R}(x)\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{alias}(x=\ast y)\SEQ e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\mathbf{AliasFail}\end{array}}

(
Rs-AliasDerefFail
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\models[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{assert}(\varphi)\SEQ e}\right\rangle\longrightarrow_{D}\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},e}\right\rangle\end{array}}

(
Rs-Assert
)

Figure 3:
Operational semantics (excerpt).

The rules formalize the informal meaning described in

\cref
sec:syntax in a straightforward manner.

•

The rules for evaluating an expression
e
that binds a variable

x

take a fresh
variable
x^{\prime}
, extend the register file
R
with the value of the right-hand side,
and rename the occurrences of
x
in the body of
e
to
x^{\prime}
to
avoid the collision of bound variable names.

•

In the rule
Rs-AddPtr
for pointer arithmetic expressions
\LET x=y\boxplus z\IN e
,
we use the meta-level operator
pv\boxplus j
, defined by:

(a,i)\boxplus j=(a,i+j)
and
\mathbf{null}\boxplus j=\mathbf{null}
.
Adding an offset to
\mathbf{null}
results in
\mathbf{null}
.

•

The rules
Rs-MkArrayIntref
and
Rs-MkArrayNestedref
are
for allocation
\LET x=\ALLOC y:\tau^{-}\TREF\IN e
.
In both rules
a
is a fresh base address and the heap
H
is extended to
H^{\prime}
so that

(a,0),\dots,(a,R(y)-1)
are all mapped to initial values that depend on
\tau^{-}
.
If
\tau^{-}
is
\mathbf{int}
, the initial values are
0
(
Rs-MkArrayIntref
);
otherwise the initial values are
\mathbf{null}
(
Rs-MkArrayNestedref
).

In the latter case, each cell is intended to be initialized later with
a pointer to another (nested) array before it is accessed.

If
\mathit{R}(y)\leq 0
, we regard

\left\{{(a,0)\mapsto 0,\dots,(a,R(y)-1)\mapsto 0}\right\}
as the empty
set, hence
\mathit{H^{\prime}}=\mathit{H}
: Although a fresh address is returned, no memory cell will be
allocated.
The operational semantics in Tanaka et
al.
[
44
]
does not include a rule for nested array allocation.

•

The two rules
Rs-AliasAddPtr
and
Rs-AliasDeref
deal with
alias
expressions.
If the two pointers compared are equal, the execution proceeds to

e
without changing
\mathit{R}
or
\mathit{H}
. Otherwise, the configuration goes to

\mathbf{AliasFail}
, which is a special erroneous configuration.

•

The rule
Rs-Assert
requires
\varphi
to be valid under
the assignment to variables according to
\mathit{R}
.

[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi
is the first-order formula obtained by substituting

R(x)
for each free variable
x
in
\varphi
if
R(x)
is an
integer. Formally,
[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi
is defined as follows:

[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi=[R(x_{1})/x_{1},\ldots,R(x_{n})/x_{n}]\,\varphi\text{ where }\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}=dom(R).

(In a well-typed program, all free variables in
\varphi
have the integer type.)
If
[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi
is not valid, the program gets stuck.

A program
\left\langle{D,e}\right\rangle
starts its execution from the configuration

\left\langle{\emptyset,\emptyset,e}\right\rangle
and reduces by using
\longrightarrow_{D}
. The
execution of an (untyped) program (1) terminates at a configuration of
the form
\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},x}\right\rangle
, representing successful termination, (2)
diverges, (3) abnormally terminates at
\mathbf{AliasFail}
, or (4) gets
stuck. There are several reasons for an execution to get stuck.

1.

Unbound variable errors, which occur when the referenced variable is not in the domain of
\mathit{R}
.

2.

Simple type errors, such as applying subtraction to a pointer, conditional branching on a pointer,
dereferencing an integer, and so on.

3.

Dereferencing the null pointer.
It occurs if
\mathit{R}(x)
is
\mathbf{null}
for a given

\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET y=\ast x\IN e}\right\rangle
or
\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},x\WRITE y\SEQ e}\right\rangle
.

4.

An out-of-bounds array access. It occurs when
\mathit{R}(x)=(a,i)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathit{H})
, for a given

\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\LET y=\ast x\IN e}\right\rangle
or
\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},x\WRITE y\SEQ e}\right\rangle
.

5.

An assertion failure. This happens when
\left\langle{\mathit{R},\mathit{H},\mathbf{assert}(\varphi)\SEQ e}\right\rangle
does not satisfy
\models[\mathit{R}]\,\varphi
.

Our type system guarantees that well-typed programs do not get stuck.

3
Type System

In this section, we give our type system to prevent assertion failures
and null/dangling pointer access and state a type soundness theorem.
The type system is based on Tanaka et al.
[
44
]
; we give a more
precise formalization of ownership functions, in particular, how
they depend on the variables in context.

3.1
Types

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\tau&\text{(types)}&::=&\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}\mid\Pi x.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\\
r&\text{(ownership terms)}&::=&\varphi\produces q,r\mid true\produces q\\
\Gamma&\text{(type environments)}&::=&x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau_{\mathit{n}}\\
\sigma&\text{(function types)}&::=&\left\langle{\Gamma}\right\rangle\ra\left\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}\mid\tau}\right\rangle\quad(\text{where }\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)=\mathit{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime}))\end{array}

Figure 4:
Syntax of types.

The syntax of
types
, ranged over by
\tau
,
ownership
terms
, ranged over by
r
, and
function types
,
ranged over by
\sigma
is shown in Figure
4
.
An integer type
\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}
represents integers satisfying a
refinement predicate
\varphi
.
Precisely,
\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}
stands for a subset of integers

i
that satisfy
[i/\nu]\varphi
.
We often write
\mathbf{int}
for
\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\top\}
.

A type of the form
\Pi x.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})
represents pointers to arrays
of type
\tau
with an ownership term
r
, explained below.
The variable
x
, which stands for an array index, is bound in
\tau
and
r
.
Thus, the element type and ownership term can depend on the array index.
Moreover, if a reference type is nested, the inner type can depend on outer array indices.
We write
|\tau|
for the simple type obtained from
\tau
in an obvious manner.

For example, let
\tau_{\mathit{ex}}:=\Pi x_{{\mathrm{1}}}.(\Pi x_{{\mathrm{2}}}.(\Pi x_{{\mathrm{3}}}.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{3}}}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{1}}}})

be a type for three-dimensional arrays of integers with ownership terms
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, and
r_{{\mathrm{3}}}
,
where
x_{1}
is the index of the outermost array,
x_{2}
that of the middle array,
and
x_{3}
that of the innermost array.
The index
x_{1}
may appear in
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}
,
r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
,
r_{{\mathrm{3}}}
, and
\varphi
;

x_{2}
may appear in
r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
,
r_{{\mathrm{3}}}
, and
\varphi
; and

x_{3}
may appear in
r_{{\mathrm{3}}}
and
\varphi
.

As in previous work
[
44
,
46
]
, the type system keeps
track of pointer
ownership
, which is represented by a rational
number q between 0 and 1 (that is,
(0\leq q\leq 1)
). The rational number
denotes the permissions for dereferencing (reading) and writing to the
element.
The ownership expresses permissions granted to pointers as follows:

•

q=1
: allows both dereferencing and writing to the element (read-write permission).

•

0<q<1
: allows dereferencing but not writing (read-only permission).

•

q=0
: allows neither reading nor writing (no
access).
1
1

1

Toman et al.
[
46
]

do
allow reading even if

q=0
. Their type system does not support pointer arithmetic
or prevent null pointer access. Thus, every pointer access is
safe (except for the null pointer). The
0
ownership is
different from positive ownership in that the refinement predicate of the element pointed to by a pointer with ownership
0
is regarded as
\top
, because the ownership being 0 means that there may be a
writable alias with ownership 1.

An ownership term, which takes the form

\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\produces q_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,\varphi_{\mathit{n}}\produces q_{\mathit{n}},true\produces q_{{\mathrm{0}}}
where each
q_{\mathit{i}}
is a
rational number such that
0\leq q_{\mathit{i}}\leq 1
, represents the
ownership of the pointer to each element of an array.
Intuitively, it means “if
\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}
is true, then the ownership is
q_{{\mathrm{1}}}
;
or else if
\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is true, then the ownership is
q_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, …”.
For example, the type

\Pi x.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,>\,x\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt(\varphi\produces 1,true\produces 0)})
where
\varphi=0\leq x\leq 2

means that the pointer has full ownership of the first three elements
but no ownership of the other elements, and that
the
i
-th element is an integer greater than
i
.
An ownership term always ends with
true\produces q
, ensuring at least one case applies.
We write
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{1}
for
true\produces 0
and
true\produces 1
, respectively, for brevity. We even write
\varphi\produces q
for
\varphi\produces q,\mathbf{0}
—when the default case returns 0.

For example, in the above type
\tau_{\mathit{ex}}
for three-dimensional arrays,
if
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(x_{1}\in[0,9]\Rightarrow 1,\mathbf{0})
,

r_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(x_{2}\in[0,x_{1}]\Rightarrow 1,\mathbf{0})
, and

r_{{\mathrm{3}}}=(x_{3}\in[0,x_{2}]\Rightarrow 1,true\produces 0)
,
then the pointer has full ownership at indices
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
,
where
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
range over the outermost, middle, and innermost indices,
and satisfy
0\leq i_{3}\leq i_{2}\leq i_{1}\leq 9
.

Type Environments.

A
type environment
, denoted
by
\Gamma
, is a sequence of type declarations
x_{\mathit{i}}\COL\tau_{\mathit{i}}
of pairwise
distinct variables. Given a type environment
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\,..\,,x_{\mathit{n}}:\tau_{\mathit{n}}
,
all the variables are bound in
\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,\tau_{\mathit{n}}
. Thus, even
mutual dependency between variables is allowed.

As defined in
\cref
sec:type-well-formedness, a well-formed type environment allows only integer variables to appear in the refinement predicates and ownership terms of other types.
We regard a type environment
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\,..\,,x_{\mathit{n}}:\tau_{\mathit{n}}
as a function that
maps each
x_{\mathit{i}}
to
\tau_{\mathit{i}}
. We write
\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
for

\left\{{x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}}\right\}
. If
x\not\in\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
, we write

\Gamma,x\COL\tau
to add a new declaration
x\COL\tau
to
\Gamma
. We write

\Gamma[x:\tau]
to signify
\Gamma(x)=\tau
and, if
x\in\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
,
write
\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\tau\right]
for a type environment
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that

\mathit{dom}(\Gamma^{\prime})=\mathit{dom}(\Gamma)
and
\Gamma^{\prime}(x)=\tau
and
\Gamma^{\prime}(y)=\Gamma(y)
for

y\neq x
.

Function Types and Function Type Environments.

A
function type

\sigma
is of the form

\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau_{\mathit{n}}}\right\rangle\ra\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{n}}\mid\tau}\right\rangle
. It means that the
function takes
x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}
of types

\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,\tau_{\mathit{n}}
, respectively, as arguments, returns
\tau
,
and changes the types of the arguments to
\tau^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{n}}

as a side effect. Formal arguments are named and can appear in

\tau_{\mathit{i}},\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}
, and
\tau
. For example, the function type

\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathbf{int},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\Pi z.(\mathbf{int}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})}\right\rangle\ra\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\mathbf{int},x_{{\mathrm{2}}}:\Pi z.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\mid\mathbf{int}}\right\rangle

where
r=(0\leq z\leq x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\Rightarrow 1)
means that the function takes
an integer
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and an integer array whose length is
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, returns an integer,
and updates the elements of the array with
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}
.

A
function type environment
, denoted by
\Theta
,
is a finite mapping from function names to function types.

Type Well-Formedness.

We enforce well-formedness conditions on types. Roughly speaking, a
type
\tau
is well-formed under type environment
\Gamma
,
written
\Gamma\vdash\tau\mbox{ ok}
, if every
predicate
\varphi
in
\tau
refers only to integer variables in
context and if the ownership of an array element is zero, its element
type is “empty”. Intuitively, a type is empty if all ownership in it is
semantically

\mathbf{0}
. For example, the type

\tau=\Pi x.(\Pi y.(\mathbf{int}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{0}}}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})
where
r=(0\leq x\leq 1\produces 1)

and
r_{{\mathrm{0}}}=(0\leq x\leq 1\land 0\leq y\leq 1\produces 1)

is well-formed but

\tau^{\prime}=\Pi x.(\Pi y.(\mathbf{int}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})

with
r^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{0}}}=(0\leq x\leq 2\land 0\leq y\leq 1\produces 1)

is not.
In
\tau
, the ownership of the element of an inner array is 1 only if
the outer array element is accessible (i.e.,
x
is either 0 or 1), whereas,
in
\tau^{\prime}
, the ownership of an element of the third inner array is 1 even if
the outer array is not accessible, violating the emptiness condition.

Formally, type well-formedness is defined by the rules in
Appendix
C.3
.

A type environment
\Gamma
is well-formed if each type declared in

\Gamma
is well-formed under
\Gamma
itself.
A function type
\left\langle{\Gamma}\right\rangle\ra\left\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}\mid\tau}\right\rangle
is well-formed if
both
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are well-formed, and
\tau
is well-formed
under
\Gamma^{\prime}
; a function type environment is well-formed if
each function type in it is well-formed.

We will assume that types, type environments, function types, and
function type environments are well-formed in judgments introduced
later and omit well-formedness conditions from derivation rules. The
type environment under which a given type is well-formed is obvious in most
cases—we will note when it is not clear.

Remark about Tanaka et al.

In Tanaka et al.
[
44
]
, an array type is decorated with an

ownership function

r
, which is a (seemingly set-theoretic)
function
from integers to
rational numbers (in the interval
[0,1]
).
The way ownership information is represented there, however, obscures
how reference types may depend on array indices and other variables,
especially when they are nested.

For example, the type of
pp
on Line
16
in Figure
1
is

\Pi x_{{\mathrm{1}}}.(\Pi x_{{\mathrm{2}}}.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{2}}}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{1}}}})

where the ownership term
r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
of the inner array type is
0\leq x_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq\texttt{n}-x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\produces 1

since the length of the array pointed to by
\mathtt{pp}\boxplus i
is
\mathtt{n}-i
.
Because
r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
depends on
x_{{\mathrm{1}}}
, it would need to be a

family
of functions, rather than a function.

3.2
Auxiliary Judgments and Encoding into Verification Logic

We often encode auxiliary judgments into formulae within the logic
used for verification. For example, we write
\Gamma\models\varphi
, which means
that formula
\varphi
is valid under
\Gamma
. This relation is
formally defined as follows:
\Gamma\models\varphi\iff\models\textit{fml}({\Gamma})\implies\varphi
,
where the function
\textit{fml}({\Gamma})
is defined by:

\displaystyle\textit{fml}({x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau_{\mathit{n}}})=\textit{fml}_{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}}({\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}})\land\cdots\land\textit{fml}_{x_{\mathit{n}}}({\tau_{\mathit{n}}})

\displaystyle\textit{fml}_{y}({\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}})=[y/\nu]\varphi\qquad\textit{fml}_{y}({\Pi x.(\tau^{\prime}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})})=\top.

The function
\textit{fml}({\Gamma})
translates refinements on integer variables into propositions, ignoring
variables of reference types.

We also encode auxiliary judgments for pointwise comparison and addition of ownership functions,
such as
\Gamma\models r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,\leq\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
\Gamma\models r_{{\mathrm{1}}}+r_{{\mathrm{2}}}\,=\,r_{{\mathrm{3}}}
into the verification logic.
Intuitively, the comparisons
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,\leq\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
and
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,=\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
for ownership terms are defined as
a pointwise extension of comparison over rational numbers. We denote
the first-order formula expressing
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,\leq\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
by

\textit{fml}({r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
. We omit the full definition for brevity. For
example, if
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}=(\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\produces q_{{\mathrm{11}}},true\produces q_{{\mathrm{12}}})
and

r_{{\mathrm{2}}}=(\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\produces q_{{\mathrm{21}}},true\produces q_{{\mathrm{22}}})
, then
\textit{fml}({r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
is

\displaystyle(\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\wedge\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\implies q_{{\mathrm{11}}}\,\leq\,q_{{\mathrm{21}}})\wedge(\neg\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\wedge\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\implies q_{{\mathrm{12}}}\,\leq\,q_{{\mathrm{21}}})

\displaystyle\land(\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\wedge\neg\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\implies q_{{\mathrm{11}}}\,\leq\,q_{{\mathrm{22}}})\wedge(\neg\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\wedge\neg\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\implies q_{{\mathrm{12}}}\,\leq\,q_{{\mathrm{22}}})

where
q_{\mathit{i}\,\mathit{j}}\,\leq\,q_{\mathit{k}\,\mathit{l}}
stands for either
\top
or
\bot
, depending
on the comparison of the two rational numbers. (Note that no rational
number will appear in the resulting formula because the comparison is
performed during the encoding.)
Then,
\Gamma\models r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,\leq\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
stands for
\models\textit{fml}({\Gamma})\implies\textit{fml}({r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
.

\textit{fml}({r_{{\mathrm{1}}}=r_{{\mathrm{2}}}})
and the encoding of pointwise addition

\textit{fml}({r_{{\mathrm{1}}}+r_{{\mathrm{2}}}=r_{{\mathrm{3}}}})
can be defined similarly.

3.3
Type System

Our type system consists of several judgment forms. The main one is
for expression typing of the form:
\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Intuitively, it means “under the environments
\Theta
and
\Gamma
,
the type of expression
e
is
\tau
, and the initial
type environment
\Gamma
is updated to
\Gamma^{\prime}
.”
As we already noted, we assume the type environments and the type are well-formed,
namely
\vdash\Gamma\mbox{ ok}
and
\vdash\Gamma^{\prime}\mbox{ ok}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\tau\mbox{ ok}
.
We often call
\Gamma
a pre-environment and
\Gamma^{\prime}
a post-environment.

Figures
5
and
6
show the
typing rules for expressions. The rules in Figure
5
are for expressions not related to heap manipulation and
are very similar to those in previous work
[
44
,
46
]
, except
for a few minor notational changes.
Standard typing rules (i.e.,
T-Int
,
T-Minus
, and
T-Let
) are omitted
from
\cref
fig:typing rules 1 for brevity; see

\cref
sec:typingOmitted.

\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[x:\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}]\vdash x:\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\produces\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\right]\end{array}}

(
T-Var
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\models\textit{Empty}({\Pi z.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mid\Gamma,x\COL\Pi z.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\vdash e:\tau\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\LET x=\mathbf{null}\IN e:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Null
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\wedge\nu\,\leq\,0\}\right]\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\wedge\nu\,>\,0\}\right]\vdash e_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[x:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\vdash\IFNP x\THEN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}\ELSE e_{{\mathrm{1}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-If
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta(f)=\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau_{\mathit{n}}}\right\rangle\ra\left\langle{x_{{\mathrm{1}}}\COL\tau^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,x_{\mathit{n}}\COL\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{n}}\mid\tau}\right\rangle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\theta=[y_{{\mathrm{1}}}/x_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,y_{\mathit{n}}/x_{\mathit{n}}]\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[y_{\mathit{i}}\hookleftarrow\theta\,\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}\right],x\COL\theta\,\tau\vdash e:\tau^{\prime}\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime\prime})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[y_{\mathit{i}}:\theta\,\tau_{\mathit{i}}]\vdash\LET x=f(y_{{\mathrm{1}}},\ldots,y_{\mathit{n}})\IN e:\tau^{\prime}\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Call
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mid\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma^{\prime\prime},\tau\leq\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime},\tau^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau^{\prime}\produces\Gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Sub
)

Figure 5:
Expression typing rules (1).

In
T-Null
, the type of

x
is given an
empty
reference type because
\mathbf{null}
is
inaccessible. The premise
\Gamma\models\textit{Empty}({\tau})
intuitively means that

\tau
is empty under
\Gamma
. Similarly to
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}\,\leq\,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}
, the type
emptiness is also encoded into a logical formula. The function

\textit{Empty}({\tau})
is defined by induction on
\tau
as follows:

\displaystyle\textit{Empty}({\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\top

\displaystyle\textit{Empty}({\Pi z.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textit{fml}({r=\mathbf{0}})\land\textit{Empty}({\tau}).

\textit{Empty}({\tau})
gathers conditions recursively if
\tau
is a nested array type.

The rule
T-If
is standard in a refinement type system. Since the

\THEN
branch (the
\ELSE
branch, resp.) is taken only when
x
is
non-positive (positive, resp.), the predicate
\varphi
for
x
is
strengthened by
\nu\,\leq\,0
(
\nu\,>\,0
, resp.).

In rule
T-Call
, substitution
\theta
renames formal parameter
names in the function type
\Theta(f)
by actual argument names. This
rule means that the types of arguments
y_{\mathit{i}}
change from

\theta\,\tau_{\mathit{i}}
to
\theta\,\tau^{\prime}_{\mathit{i}}
by the function call,
x
is given
type
\theta\,\tau
, where
\tau
is the return type.

The rule
T-Sub
is for subsumption, which strengthens the
pre-environment (
\Gamma\leq\Gamma^{\prime}
) and weakens the expression type and
the post-environment.

The full rules for subtyping are similar to those of previous work
[
44
,
46
]
and are given in

\cref
sec:subtyping.
We explain two rules here.
The rule
S-Int
for integer types:

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,\nu\colon\mathbf{int}\models\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\implies\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi_{{\mathrm{1}}}\}\leq\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi_{{\mathrm{2}}}\}\end{array}}

(
S-Int
)

requires that the predicate of a subtype has to be stronger than that
of a supertype.
The rule
S-Ref
for reference types:

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,x\COL\mathbf{int}\models r_{1}\,\geq\,r_{2}\quad\quad\Gamma,x\COL\mathbf{int}\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\Pi x.(\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{1}})\leq\Pi x.(\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{2}})\end{array}}

(
S-Ref
)

is covariant and additionally requires that the ownership term in a subtype has to
be pointwise greater than that in a supertype. Note that covariance
is safe because if an array is writable through one alias, the other
aliases will be assigned no ownership and cannot observe the update
[
9
, Section 6.3]
.

The rule
T-Var
is one of the key rules. To understand the rule,
consider, for example,
\LET y=x\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}
, which creates an alias
of
x
. A part of the ownership of
x
has to be given to

y
when typing
e_{{\mathrm{0}}}
. Such “split” of ownership is
expressed as the auxiliary judgment
\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}
—which
reads “
\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}
is split into
\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}
under
\Gamma
”
or “
\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}
and
\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}
merge into
\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}
under
\Gamma
”—for
type addition. The latter reading will be useful when we discuss the
typing rules for
\mathbf{alias}
expressions.

We show a type derivation for
\LET y=x\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}
below.
Here, we write
\tau(r)=\Pi z.(\mathbf{int}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})
and omit
\Theta
.

\frac{\displaystyle{\frac{\vdots}{\Gamma,x:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{1}}})\vdash x:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}})\produces\Gamma,x:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}})}}\quad\quad\displaystyle{\frac{\vdots}{\Gamma,x:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}}),y:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}})\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}})\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},y:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{2}}}))}}}{\Gamma,x:\tau(r_{{\mathrm{1}}})\vdash\LET y=x\IN e:\tau_{{\mathrm{0}}}\produces\Gamma^{\prime}}

where
r_{{\mathrm{1}}}=0\leq z\leq 9\produces 1
and

r_{{\mathrm{2}}}=0\leq z\leq 9\produces 0.5
.
Notice that
\Gamma\models r_{{\mathrm{2}}}+r_{{\mathrm{2}}}\,=\,r_{{\mathrm{1}}}
holds.

\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,z\COL\mathbf{int}\models r\,=\,((0\,\leq\,z\wedge z\,\leq\,y-1)\produces 1)\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}],x\COL\Pi z.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid 0\,\leq\,z\wedge z\,\leq\,y-1\implies\nu\,=\,0\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\vdash\LET x=\ALLOC y:\mathbf{int}\TREF\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-MkIntArray
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,z\COL\mathbf{int}\models r\,=\,((0\,\leq\,z\wedge z\,\leq\,y-1)\produces 1)\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,z\COL\mathbf{int}\models\textit{Empty}({\tau^{\prime}})\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}],x\COL\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt|\tau^{\prime}|=\tau^{-}\TREF\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\vdash\LET x=\ALLOC y:(\tau^{-}\TREF)\TREF\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-MkNestedArray
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\models r\,>\,\mathbf{0}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\vdash\tau^{\prime}+\tau_{x}\approx\tau_{y}\\
\Gamma,x\COL\tau_{x},z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\vdash\tau^{\prime}_{y}\approx(\tau^{\prime})^{=x}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,x\COL\tau_{x},z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,\neq\,0\}\vdash\tau^{\prime}_{y}\approx\tau_{y}\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[y\hookleftarrow\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}_{y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\right],x\COL\tau_{x}\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime\prime})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\Pi z.(\tau_{y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})]\vdash\LET x=\ast y\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Deref
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma,z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\models r\,=\,\mathbf{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash\tau^{\prime}+\tau^{\prime}_{y}\approx\tau_{y}\\
\Gamma,z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\vdash\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\approx(\tau^{\prime})^{=y}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma,z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,\neq\,0\}\vdash\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\approx\tau_{\ast x}\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\right]\left[y\hookleftarrow\tau^{\prime}_{y}\right]\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[x:\Pi z.(\tau_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})][y:\tau_{y}]\vdash x\WRITE y\SEQ e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Assign
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\vdash\Pi w.(\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}})+\Pi w.[(w-z)/w](\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})\approx\Pi w.(\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y}})\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma[z:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\left[y\hookleftarrow\Pi w.(\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y_{{\mathrm{1}}}}})\right],x\COL\Pi w.(\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces(\Gamma^{\prime},x\COL\tau^{\prime})\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[y:\Pi w.(\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y}})][z:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\vdash\LET x=y\boxplus z\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-AddPtr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{r}\Gamma\vdash(\Pi w^{\prime}.[(w^{\prime}-z)/w^{\prime}](\tau_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})+\Pi w.(\tau_{\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y}}))\approx\\
(\Pi w^{\prime}.[(w^{\prime}-z)/w^{\prime}](\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{x}}))+\Pi w.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{y}})\end{array}\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma[z:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\left[x\hookleftarrow\Pi w^{\prime}.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{x}})\right]\left[y\hookleftarrow\Pi w.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{y}})\right]\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[x:\Pi w^{\prime}.(\tau_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})][y:\Pi w.(\tau_{\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{y}})][z:\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}]\vdash\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-AliasAddPtr
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma,w\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,0\}\vdash\\
\hskip 18.49988pt(\Pi z^{\prime}.(\tau_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})+\Pi z.(\tau_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{\ast y}}))\approx(\Pi z^{\prime}.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{x}})+\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{\ast y}}))\end{array}\\
\Gamma,w\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,\neq\,0\}\vdash\Pi z.(\tau_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{\ast y}})\approx\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{\ast y}})\\
\Theta\mid\Gamma\left[x\hookleftarrow\Pi z^{\prime}.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{x}})\right]\left[y\hookleftarrow\Pi w.(\Pi z.(\tau^{\prime}_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r^{\prime}}_{\ast y}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\right]\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\\
\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma[x:\Pi z^{\prime}.(\tau_{\ast x}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{x}})][y:\Pi w.(\Pi z.(\tau_{\ast\ast y}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt{r}_{\ast y}})\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})]\vdash\mathbf{alias}(x=\ast y)\SEQ e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-AliasDeref
)

\displaystyle\frac{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Gamma\models\varphi\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\Theta\mid\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{assert}(\varphi)\SEQ e_{{\mathrm{0}}}:\tau\produces\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}}

(
T-Assert
)

Figure 6:
Expression typing rules (2).

Now, we explain the typing rules related to heap-manipulating constructs.

The rules
T-MkIntArray
and
T-MkNestedArray
are for the
creation of new arrays of integers and (nested) arrays, respectively.
In both rules, the first premise about
r
(in the type of
x
)
means that,
x
has full ownership
\Gamma\models r\,=\,\mathbf{1}
, i.e., write permission,
for the 0-th
y
elements but no ownership
\Gamma\models r\,=\,\mathbf{0}
for all the other elements.
For integer arrays, the element type asserts that all (accessible) elements are initialized to
0
.
Since nested arrays are initialized with
\mathbf{null}
, the element type
\tau^{\prime}
has to be
Empty
.

The rule
T-Deref
is for dereference
\LET x=\ast y\IN e
.
The first premise requires the ownership at
z=0
is greater
than 0, meaning that the address denoted by
y
(with offset 0)
can be read.
Since
x
becomes an alias of
\ast y
, the type of the 0th element
must be split—analogously to variable reference—and distributed between
x

and
y
after the dereference.
However, since the types of all elements have to be represented by a
single type expression
\tau_{y}
, which depends on the array index

z
, type splitting is more complicated.
The second premise means that the type
\tau_{y}
(under
z=0
)
for the 0-th element is split into
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau_{x}
, the latter of
which is given to
x
.
The third and fourth premises concern the element types
\tau^{\prime}_{y}
after
dereferencing.
They roughly mean that the 0-th element type (
\tau^{\prime}_{y}
with

z=0
) is equivalent to
\tau^{\prime}
and the other element types
(
\tau^{\prime}_{y}
with
z\neq 0
) remain the same as
\tau_{y}
.
We use another judgment
\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}
to state the two types
are
equivalent
, defined by:

\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\iff\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}\leq\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\text{ and }\Gamma\vdash\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\leq\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}.

The type
(\tau^{\prime})^{=x}
in the third premise stands for the type
obtained from
\tau^{\prime}
by adding the fact that
\ast y
is equal to

x
, if the element type is integer.
Formally,
(\tau)^{=x}
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\}^{=x}=\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\varphi\wedge(\nu\,=\,x)\}\hskip 71.13188pt\Pi z.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})^{=x}=\Pi z.(\tau\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr})\end{array}

The rule
T-Assign
handles updating an array element. The first
premise requires the ownership at
z=0
is 1, meaning that the
address denoted by
y
(with offset 0) is writable. The second,
third, and fourth premises are similar to those in
T-Deref
. Here,
the type of
y
is split into
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{y}
. The former
is used as the 0-th element type and the latter the type of
y

after the update.

The rule
T-AddPtr
for pointer arithmetic can be considered as a
generalization of typing for
\LET x=y\IN e_{{\mathrm{0}}}
discussed above
(except that the type of
y
is a reference type), in the sense
that the type of
y
before
\LET
is split into the type for

x
and
y
after
\LET
. The capture-avoiding substitution

[w-z/w]
(for logical terms for variables in formulae) means that the

n
-th element of
y
corresponds to the
(n-z)
-th element of

x
.

The rules
T-AliasAddPtr
and
T-AliasDeref
handle
\mathbf{alias}

expressions. From the viewpoint of ownership, an
\mathbf{alias}
expression
merges and re-splits the types for the aliases into two new types by using type
addition. The judgment
\Gamma\models\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{4}}}
stands for

\Gamma\models\tau_{{\mathrm{1}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{2}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{5}}}
and
\Gamma\models\tau_{{\mathrm{3}}}+\tau_{{\mathrm{4}}}\approx\tau_{{\mathrm{5}}}
for some
\tau_{{\mathrm{5}}}
.
A typical use is to transfer the predicate of one alias to another: for example, we can
derive

\Theta\mid\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}\vdash\mathbf{alias}(x=y\boxplus z)\SEQ z:\mathbf{int}\produces\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\mathrm{1}}}=x\COL\Pi w.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,w\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{1}}}}),y\COL\Pi w.(\mathbf{int}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5pt\mathbf{0}}),z\COL\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid z\,=\,1\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}=x\COL\Pi w.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,w\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{2}}}}),y\COL\Pi w.(\{\nu:\mathbf{int}\mid\nu\,=\,w+1\}\TREF^{\hskip 0.5ptr_{{\mathrm{3}}}}),z\COL\mathbf{int},

\displaystyle r_{{\mathrm{1}}}=0\leq w\leq 9\produces 1,r_{{\mathrm{2}}}=0\leq w\leq 9\produces 0.5\,\text{ and }r_{{\mathrm{3}}}=-1\leq w\leq 8\produces 0.5\ .

Here, the refinement for
y
in the post-environment
\Gamma_{{\mathrm{2}}}
is

strengthened
by telling the type system that
y\boxplus 1
is an alias of
x
.

Finally, the rule
T-Assert
requires the predicate
\varphi
to be valid under
\Gamma
.

Typing rules for function definitions and programs are the same as those in previous work
[
44
,
46
]
and are given in

\cref
sec:functionTyping.
